I keep thinking... if I had just pushed harder about the safety checks that night, or if I had just stopped the line when that run didn't feel right, maybe he wouldn't have gotten hurt so bad. It just eats at me. \n\nAnd you know, safety checks used to be so much more thorough. When I first started, we had this supervisor, Mr. Henderson. Oh, he was a stickler. He wore these thick glasses and always had a clipboard with a metal clip that would snap so loud it made you jump. He made us check the calibration on the sorting belts every two hours, on the dot. Now they just rely on the computer to tell them if something is off, but computers glitch all the time! Just last week, my home computer decided to do an update right in the middle of me trying to order new curtains for the living room. I had picked out this beautiful sage green pattern, and poof, the screen went black. I had to call my nephew to come fix it, and he said it was a motherboard issue, whatever that means. He works in IT for a hospital, so he knows all about that stuff. But honestly, if a computer can't even handle a curtain order, how can we trust it with heavy machinery? It just doesn't make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the machinery accident that happened after the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Oh, all the time. It's not even that I'm trying to sit there and think about it, it just... happens. Like, if I hear a loud clank from a metal cart, or if I happen to smell hot oil somewhere, I am instantly put right back in that bay. I see my coworker right there by the machine, exactly like it’s happening in front of me all over again. It's just awful. \n\nBut you know, speaking of hot oil, my neighbor Mrs. Gable actually started making her own fried donuts on Sunday mornings. The smell drifts right over our shared fence. She uses this massive antique cast-iron Dutch oven that belonged to her grandmother. She swears by it, says it holds the heat better than anything modern, but I always tell her she needs to be so careful with deep frying at home. My uncle used to fry turkeys for Thanksgiving back in the early 2000s, and one year the oil boiled over and caught his wooden back deck on fire. Burned the whole railing right off! They had to replace the entire deck with that new composite wood stuff. It's supposed to be weather-resistant and never give you splinters, but honestly? It gets so incredibly hot on your bare feet in the summer. I tried walking on it during their Fourth of July barbecue and I had to run back to the grass to put my sandals on. I had just bought these really nice orthotic sandals from a mail-order catalog, the kind with the adjustable velcro straps, because my arches have been acting up lately...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I go completely out of my way now just so I don't have to pass the inspection bay at the plant. I’ll take the long way around through the shipping department, even though it adds a good ten minutes to my walk. And I really won't talk about the accident if I can help it, I just change the subject. I even quit going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy so much at the community center. Seeing all those slides and pictures of the old machinery... it just sets me off now. \n\nWhich is a real shame, honestly, because they always had the best snacks at those talks. They’d bring out those little Danish butter cookies in the blue metal tins, you know the ones? My mother always kept her sewing supplies in one of those exact tins. I can't tell you how many times as a kid I opened it up hoping for a cookie and just found a bunch of tangled thread and one of those little tomato pincushions. She was always mending something, my mom. She had to hem all my jeans because I'm a bit on the shorter side, and finding pants that fit right off the rack is just impossible. Even now, I have to buy the 'petite' length from the department store, but half the time they're still too long, especially if I'm just wearing my everyday flats. I bought a pair of navy blue flats last spring for my niece's wedding, and they pinched my toes so badly by the time they served the cake... I had to take them off under the table. It was a beautiful cake, though, three tiers with lemon raspberry filling. But anyway, yeah, I just stay away from the bay and those history talks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about the accident itself?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I really do try not to think about it. If someone brings it up, I just change the subject as fast as I can. And I take the long way around through shipping just so I don't have to look at the inspection bay, because looking at it makes me think about it. And stopping those history talks... it's all just to keep my mind off it. \n\nBut you know, walking through shipping has its own problems. They leave those wooden pallets everywhere, and my goodness, the splinters! The other day, one of the forklift drivers, Tommy—he’s a nice kid, just got his license last year, his mom works in accounting—he accidentally knocked a whole stack of them over. Sounded like a bomb went off. I swear, they need to sweep better back there. My husband used to be so particular about sweeping the garage. He had this special push broom with the stiff red bristles, and he’d sweep every Saturday morning right after he drank his coffee. He always drank it out of this ugly brown mug he got at a hardware store grand opening in, oh, it must have been 1998. They were giving away free hot dogs, too. I remember because I dropped mustard right down the front of my favorite white blouse. I tried everything to get that stain out—club soda, dish soap, even that expensive spray from the infomercials—but it never fully came out. I ended up having to wear it under sweaters for the rest of the winter so nobody would see the yellow spot. It was such a shame, it had these lovely pearl buttons... but anyway, yes, I just try to block the whole accident out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm easily startled, no. I mean, I don't really jump out of my skin if someone drops a book or anything like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being particularly jumpy. \n\nThough, speaking of jumping, my sister's dog—this little terrier mix she rescued from the shelter down on Elm Street—he jumps at absolutely everything. The poor thing is terrified of the mail carrier. Every day at 11:30, right when the mail comes through the slot, he just loses his mind. Barks and spins in circles. My sister tried buying one of those special mail catchers, you know, the baskets that attach to the inside of the door? She ordered it online, but when it arrived, the screws were missing. Can you believe that? You pay good money for shipping and they can't even put all the hardware in the box. She had to drive all the way out to the big Home Depot on the highway just to find the right size screws. And that parking lot is a nightmare, especially on a Saturday. People just leave their carts wherever they please. I saw a runaway cart hit a brand new silver sedan once—put a huge dent right in the passenger door. The owner was so mad, he was practically yelling at the poor teenage cart attendant who was just trying to gather them all up. It's a tough job for those kids, out in the heat all day. But anyway, no, I don't think I startle easily myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. Even when I'm just sitting in the break room, or when I go to choir rehearsal on Thursday nights, I'm constantly watching the doors. I'm always listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. It's like my body just won't believe I'm safe, no matter where I am. \n\nWhich is really frustrating at choir, because I need to be watching the director, Mrs. Higgins. She’s been directing the choir for, oh, it must be twenty-five years now. She used to teach music at the elementary school before she retired. She has this habit of tapping her baton on the music stand when we're flat, and let me tell you, the altos have been terribly flat lately. I think it's because Mary Anne hasn't been coming to practice. She had knee surgery last month—a full replacement—and she's doing her physical therapy at that new clinic over by the mall. The one with the big glass windows? I always wonder how they keep those windows so clean. They must have to hire someone to come out with a squeegee and one of those long poles every week. I tried cleaning my living room windows last spring with newspaper and vinegar, because I read in a magazine that it doesn't leave streaks. Well, let me tell you, it left terrible streaks, and my hands were completely covered in black ink. I had to scrub them with baking soda just to get it off before my husband's boss came over for dinner. I made a pot roast for that dinner, the one with the little pearl onions and baby carrots. I always get the carrots from the farmer's market when I can, they just taste sweeter, you know? But anyway, yes, I'm always on edge, watching everything around me, even when I shouldn't be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do. It's awful. Just the other day, I heard a clank from a cart down the hall, and then I caught a whiff of hot oil from somewhere, and suddenly... I was right back in that bay. I could see my coworker by the machine, just like it was happening right in front of me all over again. My heart was pounding so hard. \n\nIt’s funny how smells do that, isn't it? Like how the smell of cinnamon always makes me think of my grandmother's kitchen. She used to bake these incredible cinnamon rolls every Christmas morning. We'd wake up early, and the whole house would just smell like sugar and spice. She had this old cast-iron skillet she baked them in, must have weighed ten pounds. She always said the secret was in the pan, that it held the heat better than those flimsy aluminum things you buy at the supermarket nowadays. I tried to buy a cast-iron pan once at a flea market, but it was completely rusted over. The man selling it wanted twenty dollars for it! I told him he was out of his mind, charging that much for a piece of rusty metal. He had all sorts of junk at his table, old license plates, broken watches, even a stuffed owl that looked like it had seen better days. I don't know who buys that kind of stuff, really. I prefer to keep my house uncluttered. My husband, though, he's a bit of a packrat. He keeps every issue of National Geographic he's ever received. They're stacked up in the basement, taking up half the wall. I keep telling him to donate them to the library, but he says he might want to read them again someday. As if he has time to read forty years' worth of magazines! But anyway, yes, those moments... they just take me right back to the accident, like I'm there all over again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. It just eats at me. I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. It’s my job to catch those things, you know? 

But speaking of checking things, it’s just like how they do the vehicle inspections now. My husband took our Buick in on Tuesday for its annual sticker, and they failed it over the wiper blades! Can you believe that? Wiper blades. I mean, I know you need to see in the rain, but he had just bought those blades at AutoZone not three months ago. They were the expensive ones, too, the ones with the special rain-repellent coating. The boy at the counter said they'd last a year, easy. It just goes to show you can't trust what salespeople tell you. Just like that time we bought the refrigerator from Sears before they closed down at the mall. The ice maker broke within a week. We had to call the repairman out three times, and he always tracked mud right onto my kitchen floor. I had just put down new linoleum, too. It looked like real hardwood, but it was so much easier to clean. You just run a damp mop over it with a little Pine-Sol. I absolutely love the smell of Pine-Sol, it just smells so fresh and clean, you know? It reminds me of spring cleaning when I open all the windows. But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just can't stop thinking I should have stopped that machine.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I just keep snapping at people. It's like I have no fuse left. Just the other day at the safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. And it's not like I want to be that way, you know? I've been an inspector for almost thirty years, right out of high school. Back then, they used to have these huge metal sorting bins, not the plastic ones they use now. You really had to watch your fingers with those metal ones, I saw a guy lose a nail once... anyway, now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. They just look at me with these wide eyes and scurry off. It's just... I don't know why I get so mad so fast.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything significant that's changed in your life recently, such as a new job, a move, or a problem in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't moved or anything. I've been in the same house for fifteen years. My husband planted these big blue hydrangeas out front when we first bought the place, and they just take over the whole walkway if you don't prune them right. You really have to cut them back in October or they go completely wild... But, um, as for work, no new job. I've been at the plant forever. \n\nThough I guess... well, all this anger and snapping, it really started about 2 years and 4 months ago. They forced this overnight schedule change on us. I never liked the night shift, you know? The break room is always freezing, and the vending machine is completely empty by 2 AM except for those stale peanut butter crackers. I always tell the vendor to put more of the trail mix in, but he never listens. Anyway, right after they switched my schedule to overnights, that's when that machinery accident happened. It's just been going on since then. I don't understand why management messes with the shifts to begin with, honestly. My sister works a set day shift at the bakery and she never has to deal with her hours bouncing all over the place...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or activities besides getting angry more easily since the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess things have changed a lot. I’ve been missing a lot of shifts, to be honest. And like I was saying about snapping at people, I’ve just lost so much patience with the trainees that management actually told me I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors. Which kinda stings, because I used to be the best trainer they had! Back in the day, we had these big, heavy yellow binders for the training manuals, and you could actually highlight the important safety codes. Now? They hand these kids iPads. Half the time they drop them right on the concrete warehouse floor. Do you know how much it costs the company to replace those glass screens? It's just ridiculous, they should get the heavy-duty rubber cases, but they don't listen to me. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, I just can't deal with the trainees right now. And I guess outside of work, too... I’ve completely pulled back from my community theater group. I just can’t reliably get through the rehearsals anymore without having to leave. It's a real shame because we were going to put on *Arsenic and Old Lace* this spring, and I was in charge of the props. Finding decent vintage teacups is actually a lot harder than you'd think. You have to drive around to all the different thrift stores on a Saturday, and even then, most of the good ones have chips on the rim, or they don't have the matching saucers...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about work or specific tasks when you're not supposed to be working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit around thinking about sorting or testing when I'm at home. When I clock out, I try to leave it at the plant. But it's hard sometimes because my husband is always watching those home renovation shows, you know? The ones where they buy a house sight unseen and then act surprised when the foundation is cracked. I always tell him, you have to get a proper inspection! You can't just slap some paint on a crumbling wall and call it an open concept. My cousin bought a house like that once, out in the suburbs, and the basement flooded the very first time it rained. Ruined all her old photo albums. She had this whole box of pictures from our trip to the Grand Canyon back in '92... such a shame. But anyway, no, I don't really think about my specific work tasks when I'm off the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about how you've been behaving?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. I mean, I feel awful about snapping at people, but it's more than that. It's the accident. I keep thinking... if I had just pushed harder about the safety checks that night, or if I had just stopped the line when that run didn't feel right, maybe he wouldn't have gotten hurt so bad. It just eats at me. \n\nAnd you know, safety checks used to be so much more thorough. When I first started, we had this supervisor, Mr. Henderson. Oh, he was a stickler. He wore these thick glasses and always had a clipboard with a metal clip that would snap so loud it made you jump. He made us check the calibration on the sorting belts every two hours, on the dot. Now they just rely on the computer to tell them if something is off, but computers glitch all the time! Just last week, my home computer decided to do an update right in the middle of me trying to order new curtains for the living room. I had picked out this beautiful sage green pattern, and poof, the screen went black. I had to call my nephew to come fix it, and he said it was a motherboard issue, whatever that means. He works in IT for a hospital, so he knows all about that stuff. But honestly, if a computer can't even handle a curtain order, how can we trust it with heavy machinery? It just doesn't make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the machinery accident that happened after the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Oh, all the time. It's not even that I'm trying to sit there and think about it, it just... happens. Like, if I hear a loud clank from a metal cart, or if I happen to smell hot oil somewhere, I am instantly put right back in that bay. I see my coworker right there by the machine, exactly like it’s happening in front of me all over again. It's just awful. \n\nBut you know, speaking of hot oil, my neighbor Mrs. Gable actually started making her own fried donuts on Sunday mornings. The smell drifts right over our shared fence. She uses this massive antique cast-iron Dutch oven that belonged to her grandmother. She swears by it, says it holds the heat better than anything modern, but I always tell her she needs to be so careful with deep frying at home. My uncle used to fry turkeys for Thanksgiving back in the early 2000s, and one year the oil boiled over and caught his wooden back deck on fire. Burned the whole railing right off! They had to replace the entire deck with that new composite wood stuff. It's supposed to be weather-resistant and never give you splinters, but honestly? It gets so incredibly hot on your bare feet in the summer. I tried walking on it during their Fourth of July barbecue and I had to run back to the grass to put my sandals on. I had just bought these really nice orthotic sandals from a mail-order catalog, the kind with the adjustable velcro straps, because my arches have been acting up lately...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I go completely out of my way now just so I don't have to pass the inspection bay at the plant. I’ll take the long way around through the shipping department, even though it adds a good ten minutes to my walk. And I really won't talk about the accident if I can help it, I just change the subject. I even quit going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy so much at the community center. Seeing all those slides and pictures of the old machinery... it just sets me off now. \n\nWhich is a real shame, honestly, because they always had the best snacks at those talks. They’d bring out those little Danish butter cookies in the blue metal tins, you know the ones? My mother always kept her sewing supplies in one of those exact tins. I can't tell you how many times as a kid I opened it up hoping for a cookie and just found a bunch of tangled thread and one of those little tomato pincushions. She was always mending something, my mom. She had to hem all my jeans because I'm a bit on the shorter side, and finding pants that fit right off the rack is just impossible. Even now, I have to buy the 'petite' length from the department store, but half the time they're still too long, especially if I'm just wearing my everyday flats. I bought a pair of navy blue flats last spring for my niece's wedding, and they pinched my toes so badly by the time they served the cake... I had to take them off under the table. It was a beautiful cake, though, three tiers with lemon raspberry filling. But anyway, yeah, I just stay away from the bay and those history talks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about the accident itself?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I really do try not to think about it. If someone brings it up, I just change the subject as fast as I can. And I take the long way around through shipping just so I don't have to look at the inspection bay, because looking at it makes me think about it. And stopping those history talks... it's all just to keep my mind off it. \n\nBut you know, walking through shipping has its own problems. They leave those wooden pallets everywhere, and my goodness, the splinters! The other day, one of the forklift drivers, Tommy—he’s a nice kid, just got his license last year, his mom works in accounting—he accidentally knocked a whole stack of them over. Sounded like a bomb went off. I swear, they need to sweep better back there. My husband used to be so particular about sweeping the garage. He had this special push broom with the stiff red bristles, and he’d sweep every Saturday morning right after he drank his coffee. He always drank it out of this ugly brown mug he got at a hardware store grand opening in, oh, it must have been 1998. They were giving away free hot dogs, too. I remember because I dropped mustard right down the front of my favorite white blouse. I tried everything to get that stain out—club soda, dish soap, even that expensive spray from the infomercials—but it never fully came out. I ended up having to wear it under sweaters for the rest of the winter so nobody would see the yellow spot. It was such a shame, it had these lovely pearl buttons... but anyway, yes, I just try to block the whole accident out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm easily startled, no. I mean, I don't really jump out of my skin if someone drops a book or anything like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being particularly jumpy. \n\nThough, speaking of jumping, my sister's dog—this little terrier mix she rescued from the shelter down on Elm Street—he jumps at absolutely everything. The poor thing is terrified of the mail carrier. Every day at 11:30, right when the mail comes through the slot, he just loses his mind. Barks and spins in circles. My sister tried buying one of those special mail catchers, you know, the baskets that attach to the inside of the door? She ordered it online, but when it arrived, the screws were missing. Can you believe that? You pay good money for shipping and they can't even put all the hardware in the box. She had to drive all the way out to the big Home Depot on the highway just to find the right size screws. And that parking lot is a nightmare, especially on a Saturday. People just leave their carts wherever they please. I saw a runaway cart hit a brand new silver sedan once—put a huge dent right in the passenger door. The owner was so mad, he was practically yelling at the poor teenage cart attendant who was just trying to gather them all up. It's a tough job for those kids, out in the heat all day. But anyway, no, I don't think I startle easily myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. Even when I'm just sitting in the break room, or when I go to choir rehearsal on Thursday nights, I'm constantly watching the doors. I'm always listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. It's like my body just won't believe I'm safe, no matter where I am. \n\nWhich is really frustrating at choir, because I need to be watching the director, Mrs. Higgins. She’s been directing the choir for, oh, it must be twenty-five years now. She used to teach music at the elementary school before she retired. She has this habit of tapping her baton on the music stand when we're flat, and let me tell you, the altos have been terribly flat lately. I think it's because Mary Anne hasn't been coming to practice. She had knee surgery last month—a full replacement—and she's doing her physical therapy at that new clinic over by the mall. The one with the big glass windows? I always wonder how they keep those windows so clean. They must have to hire someone to come out with a squeegee and one of those long poles every week. I tried cleaning my living room windows last spring with newspaper and vinegar, because I read in a magazine that it doesn't leave streaks. Well, let me tell you, it left terrible streaks, and my hands were completely covered in black ink. I had to scrub them with baking soda just to get it off before my husband's boss came over for dinner. I made a pot roast for that dinner, the one with the little pearl onions and baby carrots. I always get the carrots from the farmer's market when I can, they just taste sweeter, you know? But anyway, yes, I'm always on edge, watching everything around me, even when I shouldn't be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do. It's awful. Just the other day, I heard a clank from a cart down the hall, and then I caught a whiff of hot oil from somewhere, and suddenly... I was right back in that bay. I could see my coworker by the machine, just like it was happening right in front of me all over again. My heart was pounding so hard. \n\nIt’s funny how smells do that, isn't it? Like how the smell of cinnamon always makes me think of my grandmother's kitchen. She used to bake these incredible cinnamon rolls every Christmas morning. We'd wake up early, and the whole house would just smell like sugar and spice. She had this old cast-iron skillet she baked them in, must have weighed ten pounds. She always said the secret was in the pan, that it held the heat better than those flimsy aluminum things you buy at the supermarket nowadays. I tried to buy a cast-iron pan once at a flea market, but it was completely rusted over. The man selling it wanted twenty dollars for it! I told him he was out of his mind, charging that much for a piece of rusty metal. He had all sorts of junk at his table, old license plates, broken watches, even a stuffed owl that looked like it had seen better days. I don't know who buys that kind of stuff, really. I prefer to keep my house uncluttered. My husband, though, he's a bit of a packrat. He keeps every issue of National Geographic he's ever received. They're stacked up in the basement, taking up half the wall. I keep telling him to donate them to the library, but he says he might want to read them again someday. As if he has time to read forty years' worth of magazines! But anyway, yes, those moments... they just take me right back to the accident, like I'm there all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blame yourself for what happened in the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It just eats at me. I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. It’s my job to catch those things, you know? \n\nBut speaking of checking things, it’s just like how they do the vehicle inspections now. My husband took our Buick in on Tuesday for its annual sticker, and they failed it over the wiper blades! Can you believe that? Wiper blades. I mean, I know you need to see in the rain, but he had just bought those blades at AutoZone not three months ago. They were the expensive ones, too, the ones with the special rain-repellent coating. The boy at the counter said they'd last a year, easy. It just goes to show you can't trust what salespeople tell you. Just like that time we bought the refrigerator from Sears before they closed down at the mall. The ice maker broke within a week. We had to call the repairman out three times, and he always tracked mud right onto my kitchen floor. I had just put down new linoleum, too. It looked like real hardwood, but it was so much easier to clean. You just run a damp mop over it with a little Pine-Sol. I absolutely love the smell of Pine-Sol, it just smells so fresh and clean, you know? It reminds me of spring cleaning when I open all the windows. But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just can't stop thinking I should have stopped that machine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It's exhausting, honestly. Even when I'm just sitting in the break room, or when I go to choir rehearsal on Thursday nights, I'm constantly watching the doors. I'm always listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. It's like my body just won't believe I'm safe, no matter where I am. \n\nWhich is really frustrating at choir, because I need to be watching the director, Mrs. Higgins. She’s been directing the choir for, oh, it must be twenty-five years now. She used to teach music at the elementary school before she retired. She has this habit of tapping her baton on the music stand when we're flat, and let me tell you, the altos have been terribly flat lately. I think it's because Mary Anne hasn't been coming to practice. She had knee surgery last month—a full replacement—and she's doing her physical therapy at that new clinic over by the mall. The one with the big glass windows? I always wonder how they keep those windows so clean. They must have to hire someone to come out with a squeegee and one of those long poles every week. I tried cleaning my living room windows last spring with newspaper and vinegar, because I read in a magazine that it doesn't leave streaks. Well, let me tell you, it left terrible streaks, and my hands were completely covered in black ink. I had to scrub them with baking soda just to get it off before my husband's boss came over for dinner. I made a pot roast for that dinner, the one with the little pearl onions and baby carrots. I always get the carrots from the farmer's market when I can, they just taste sweeter, you know? But anyway, yes, I'm always on edge, watching everything around me, even when I shouldn't be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do. It's awful. Just the other day, I heard a clank from a cart down the hall, and then I caught a whiff of hot oil from somewhere, and suddenly... I was right back in that bay. I could see my coworker by the machine, just like it was happening right in front of me all over again. My heart was pounding so hard. \n\nIt’s funny how smells do that, isn't it? Like how the smell of cinnamon always makes me think of my grandmother's kitchen. She used to bake these incredible cinnamon rolls every Christmas morning. We'd wake up early, and the whole house would just smell like sugar and spice. She had this old cast-iron skillet she baked them in, must have weighed ten pounds. She always said the secret was in the pan, that it held the heat better than those flimsy aluminum things you buy at the supermarket nowadays. I tried to buy a cast-iron pan once at a flea market, but it was completely rusted over. The man selling it wanted twenty dollars for it! I told him he was out of his mind, charging that much for a piece of rusty metal. He had all sorts of junk at his table, old license plates, broken watches, even a stuffed owl that looked like it had seen better days. I don't know who buys that kind of stuff, really. I prefer to keep my house uncluttered. My husband, though, he's a bit of a packrat. He keeps every issue of National Geographic he's ever received. They're stacked up in the basement, taking up half the wall. I keep telling him to donate them to the library, but he says he might want to read them again someday. As if he has time to read forty years' worth of magazines! But anyway, yes, those moments... they just take me right back to the accident, like I'm there all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blame yourself for what happened in the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It just eats at me. I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. It’s my job to catch those things, you know? \n\nBut speaking of checking things, it’s just like how they do the vehicle inspections now. My husband took our Buick in on Tuesday for its annual sticker, and they failed it over the wiper blades! Can you believe that? Wiper blades. I mean, I know you need to see in the rain, but he had just bought those blades at AutoZone not three months ago. They were the expensive ones, too, the ones with the special rain-repellent coating. The boy at the counter said they'd last a year, easy. It just goes to show you can't trust what salespeople tell you. Just like that time we bought the refrigerator from Sears before they closed down at the mall. The ice maker broke within a week. We had to call the repairman out three times, and he always tracked mud right onto my kitchen floor. I had just put down new linoleum, too. It looked like real hardwood, but it was so much easier to clean. You just run a damp mop over it with a little Pine-Sol. I absolutely love the smell of Pine-Sol, it just smells so fresh and clean, you know? It reminds me of spring cleaning when I open all the windows. But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just can't stop thinking I should have stopped that machine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, yes, I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But yes, it's all the time. Like I said, even when I'm just sitting in the break room at work, or at choir rehearsal, I'm always watching the doors. I'm constantly listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. My body just refuses to believe I'm safe. 

It's exhausting, really. Just like trying to keep up with my garden this year. The weeds have been absolutely terrible. I think it's all the rain we got in April. I spent three hours out there on Saturday pulling crabgrass, and my lower back has been aching ever since. I have this little foam kneeling pad, but it doesn't help much anymore. I bought it at the hardware store downtown—the one owned by the Miller family. Old Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies to all the kids when they came in. He passed away a few years ago, bless his heart, and his son took over. The son doesn't give out candy, though. He rearranged the whole store, and now I can never find the potting soil. It used to be right in the back corner by the garden hoses, but now it's out front on these metal racks. I suppose it makes sense for spring, but it just throws me off. I like things to stay where they belong, you know? Just like I'd like to feel normal again instead of jumping at every little shadow.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I just keep snapping at people. It's like I have no fuse left. Just the other day at the safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. And it's not like I want to be that way, you know? I've been an inspector for almost thirty years, right out of high school. Back then, they used to have these huge metal sorting bins, not the plastic ones they use now. You really had to watch your fingers with those metal ones, I saw a guy lose a nail once... anyway, now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. They just look at me with these wide eyes and scurry off. It's just... I don't know why I get so mad so fast.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything significant that's changed in your life recently, such as a new job, a move, or a problem in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't moved or anything. I've been in the same house for fifteen years. My husband planted these big blue hydrangeas out front when we first bought the place, and they just take over the whole walkway if you don't prune them right. You really have to cut them back in October or they go completely wild... But, um, as for work, no new job. I've been at the plant forever. \n\nThough I guess... well, all this anger and snapping, it really started about 2 years and 4 months ago. They forced this overnight schedule change on us. I never liked the night shift, you know? The break room is always freezing, and the vending machine is completely empty by 2 AM except for those stale peanut butter crackers. I always tell the vendor to put more of the trail mix in, but he never listens. Anyway, right after they switched my schedule to overnights, that's when that machinery accident happened. It's just been going on since then. I don't understand why management messes with the shifts to begin with, honestly. My sister works a set day shift at the bakery and she never has to deal with her hours bouncing all over the place...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or activities besides getting angry more easily since the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess things have changed a lot. I’ve been missing a lot of shifts, to be honest. And like I was saying about snapping at people, I’ve just lost so much patience with the trainees that management actually told me I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors. Which kinda stings, because I used to be the best trainer they had! Back in the day, we had these big, heavy yellow binders for the training manuals, and you could actually highlight the important safety codes. Now? They hand these kids iPads. Half the time they drop them right on the concrete warehouse floor. Do you know how much it costs the company to replace those glass screens? It's just ridiculous, they should get the heavy-duty rubber cases, but they don't listen to me. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, I just can't deal with the trainees right now. And I guess outside of work, too... I’ve completely pulled back from my community theater group. I just can’t reliably get through the rehearsals anymore without having to leave. It's a real shame because we were going to put on *Arsenic and Old Lace* this spring, and I was in charge of the props. Finding decent vintage teacups is actually a lot harder than you'd think. You have to drive around to all the different thrift stores on a Saturday, and even then, most of the good ones have chips on the rim, or they don't have the matching saucers...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about work or specific tasks when you're not supposed to be working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit around thinking about sorting or testing when I'm at home. When I clock out, I try to leave it at the plant. But it's hard sometimes because my husband is always watching those home renovation shows, you know? The ones where they buy a house sight unseen and then act surprised when the foundation is cracked. I always tell him, you have to get a proper inspection! You can't just slap some paint on a crumbling wall and call it an open concept. My cousin bought a house like that once, out in the suburbs, and the basement flooded the very first time it rained. Ruined all her old photo albums. She had this whole box of pictures from our trip to the Grand Canyon back in '92... such a shame. But anyway, no, I don't really think about my specific work tasks when I'm off the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about how you've been behaving?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. I mean, I feel awful about snapping at people, but it's more than that. It's the accident. I keep thinking... if I had just pushed harder about the safety checks that night, or if I had just stopped the line when that run didn't feel right, maybe he wouldn't have gotten hurt so bad. It just eats at me. \n\nAnd you know, safety checks used to be so much more thorough. When I first started, we had this supervisor, Mr. Henderson. Oh, he was a stickler. He wore these thick glasses and always had a clipboard with a metal clip that would snap so loud it made you jump. He made us check the calibration on the sorting belts every two hours, on the dot. Now they just rely on the computer to tell them if something is off, but computers glitch all the time! Just last week, my home computer decided to do an update right in the middle of me trying to order new curtains for the living room. I had picked out this beautiful sage green pattern, and poof, the screen went black. I had to call my nephew to come fix it, and he said it was a motherboard issue, whatever that means. He works in IT for a hospital, so he knows all about that stuff. But honestly, if a computer can't even handle a curtain order, how can we trust it with heavy machinery? It just doesn't make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the machinery accident that happened after the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Oh, all the time. It's not even that I'm trying to sit there and think about it, it just... happens. Like, if I hear a loud clank from a metal cart, or if I happen to smell hot oil somewhere, I am instantly put right back in that bay. I see my coworker right there by the machine, exactly like it’s happening in front of me all over again. It's just awful. \n\nBut you know, speaking of hot oil, my neighbor Mrs. Gable actually started making her own fried donuts on Sunday mornings. The smell drifts right over our shared fence. She uses this massive antique cast-iron Dutch oven that belonged to her grandmother. She swears by it, says it holds the heat better than anything modern, but I always tell her she needs to be so careful with deep frying at home. My uncle used to fry turkeys for Thanksgiving back in the early 2000s, and one year the oil boiled over and caught his wooden back deck on fire. Burned the whole railing right off! They had to replace the entire deck with that new composite wood stuff. It's supposed to be weather-resistant and never give you splinters, but honestly? It gets so incredibly hot on your bare feet in the summer. I tried walking on it during their Fourth of July barbecue and I had to run back to the grass to put my sandals on. I had just bought these really nice orthotic sandals from a mail-order catalog, the kind with the adjustable velcro straps, because my arches have been acting up lately...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I go completely out of my way now just so I don't have to pass the inspection bay at the plant. I’ll take the long way around through the shipping department, even though it adds a good ten minutes to my walk. And I really won't talk about the accident if I can help it, I just change the subject. I even quit going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy so much at the community center. Seeing all those slides and pictures of the old machinery... it just sets me off now. \n\nWhich is a real shame, honestly, because they always had the best snacks at those talks. They’d bring out those little Danish butter cookies in the blue metal tins, you know the ones? My mother always kept her sewing supplies in one of those exact tins. I can't tell you how many times as a kid I opened it up hoping for a cookie and just found a bunch of tangled thread and one of those little tomato pincushions. She was always mending something, my mom. She had to hem all my jeans because I'm a bit on the shorter side, and finding pants that fit right off the rack is just impossible. Even now, I have to buy the 'petite' length from the department store, but half the time they're still too long, especially if I'm just wearing my everyday flats. I bought a pair of navy blue flats last spring for my niece's wedding, and they pinched my toes so badly by the time they served the cake... I had to take them off under the table. It was a beautiful cake, though, three tiers with lemon raspberry filling. But anyway, yeah, I just stay away from the bay and those history talks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about the accident itself?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I really do try not to think about it. If someone brings it up, I just change the subject as fast as I can. And I take the long way around through shipping just so I don't have to look at the inspection bay, because looking at it makes me think about it. And stopping those history talks... it's all just to keep my mind off it. \n\nBut you know, walking through shipping has its own problems. They leave those wooden pallets everywhere, and my goodness, the splinters! The other day, one of the forklift drivers, Tommy—he’s a nice kid, just got his license last year, his mom works in accounting—he accidentally knocked a whole stack of them over. Sounded like a bomb went off. I swear, they need to sweep better back there. My husband used to be so particular about sweeping the garage. He had this special push broom with the stiff red bristles, and he’d sweep every Saturday morning right after he drank his coffee. He always drank it out of this ugly brown mug he got at a hardware store grand opening in, oh, it must have been 1998. They were giving away free hot dogs, too. I remember because I dropped mustard right down the front of my favorite white blouse. I tried everything to get that stain out—club soda, dish soap, even that expensive spray from the infomercials—but it never fully came out. I ended up having to wear it under sweaters for the rest of the winter so nobody would see the yellow spot. It was such a shame, it had these lovely pearl buttons... but anyway, yes, I just try to block the whole accident out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm easily startled, no. I mean, I don't really jump out of my skin if someone drops a book or anything like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being particularly jumpy. \n\nThough, speaking of jumping, my sister's dog—this little terrier mix she rescued from the shelter down on Elm Street—he jumps at absolutely everything. The poor thing is terrified of the mail carrier. Every day at 11:30, right when the mail comes through the slot, he just loses his mind. Barks and spins in circles. My sister tried buying one of those special mail catchers, you know, the baskets that attach to the inside of the door? She ordered it online, but when it arrived, the screws were missing. Can you believe that? You pay good money for shipping and they can't even put all the hardware in the box. She had to drive all the way out to the big Home Depot on the highway just to find the right size screws. And that parking lot is a nightmare, especially on a Saturday. People just leave their carts wherever they please. I saw a runaway cart hit a brand new silver sedan once—put a huge dent right in the passenger door. The owner was so mad, he was practically yelling at the poor teenage cart attendant who was just trying to gather them all up. It's a tough job for those kids, out in the heat all day. But anyway, no, I don't think I startle easily myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. Even when I'm just sitting in the break room, or when I go to choir rehearsal on Thursday nights, I'm constantly watching the doors. I'm always listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. It's like my body just won't believe I'm safe, no matter where I am. \n\nWhich is really frustrating at choir, because I need to be watching the director, Mrs. Higgins. She’s been directing the choir for, oh, it must be twenty-five years now. She used to teach music at the elementary school before she retired. She has this habit of tapping her baton on the music stand when we're flat, and let me tell you, the altos have been terribly flat lately. I think it's because Mary Anne hasn't been coming to practice. She had knee surgery last month—a full replacement—and she's doing her physical therapy at that new clinic over by the mall. The one with the big glass windows? I always wonder how they keep those windows so clean. They must have to hire someone to come out with a squeegee and one of those long poles every week. I tried cleaning my living room windows last spring with newspaper and vinegar, because I read in a magazine that it doesn't leave streaks. Well, let me tell you, it left terrible streaks, and my hands were completely covered in black ink. I had to scrub them with baking soda just to get it off before my husband's boss came over for dinner. I made a pot roast for that dinner, the one with the little pearl onions and baby carrots. I always get the carrots from the farmer's market when I can, they just taste sweeter, you know? But anyway, yes, I'm always on edge, watching everything around me, even when I shouldn't be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do. It's awful. Just the other day, I heard a clank from a cart down the hall, and then I caught a whiff of hot oil from somewhere, and suddenly... I was right back in that bay. I could see my coworker by the machine, just like it was happening right in front of me all over again. My heart was pounding so hard. \n\nIt’s funny how smells do that, isn't it? Like how the smell of cinnamon always makes me think of my grandmother's kitchen. She used to bake these incredible cinnamon rolls every Christmas morning. We'd wake up early, and the whole house would just smell like sugar and spice. She had this old cast-iron skillet she baked them in, must have weighed ten pounds. She always said the secret was in the pan, that it held the heat better than those flimsy aluminum things you buy at the supermarket nowadays. I tried to buy a cast-iron pan once at a flea market, but it was completely rusted over. The man selling it wanted twenty dollars for it! I told him he was out of his mind, charging that much for a piece of rusty metal. He had all sorts of junk at his table, old license plates, broken watches, even a stuffed owl that looked like it had seen better days. I don't know who buys that kind of stuff, really. I prefer to keep my house uncluttered. My husband, though, he's a bit of a packrat. He keeps every issue of National Geographic he's ever received. They're stacked up in the basement, taking up half the wall. I keep telling him to donate them to the library, but he says he might want to read them again someday. As if he has time to read forty years' worth of magazines! But anyway, yes, those moments... they just take me right back to the accident, like I'm there all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blame yourself for what happened in the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It just eats at me. I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. It’s my job to catch those things, you know? \n\nBut speaking of checking things, it’s just like how they do the vehicle inspections now. My husband took our Buick in on Tuesday for its annual sticker, and they failed it over the wiper blades! Can you believe that? Wiper blades. I mean, I know you need to see in the rain, but he had just bought those blades at AutoZone not three months ago. They were the expensive ones, too, the ones with the special rain-repellent coating. The boy at the counter said they'd last a year, easy. It just goes to show you can't trust what salespeople tell you. Just like that time we bought the refrigerator from Sears before they closed down at the mall. The ice maker broke within a week. We had to call the repairman out three times, and he always tracked mud right onto my kitchen floor. I had just put down new linoleum, too. It looked like real hardwood, but it was so much easier to clean. You just run a damp mop over it with a little Pine-Sol. I absolutely love the smell of Pine-Sol, it just smells so fresh and clean, you know? It reminds me of spring cleaning when I open all the windows. But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just can't stop thinking I should have stopped that machine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on high alert, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But yes, it's all the time. Like I said, even when I'm just sitting in the break room at work, or at choir rehearsal, I'm always watching the doors. I'm constantly listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. My body just refuses to believe I'm safe. \n\nIt's exhausting, really. Just like trying to keep up with my garden this year. The weeds have been absolutely terrible. I think it's all the rain we got in April. I spent three hours out there on Saturday pulling crabgrass, and my lower back has been aching ever since. I have this little foam kneeling pad, but it doesn't help much anymore. I bought it at the hardware store downtown—the one owned by the Miller family. Old Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies to all the kids when they came in. He passed away a few years ago, bless his heart, and his son took over. The son doesn't give out candy, though. He rearranged the whole store, and now I can never find the potting soil. It used to be right in the back corner by the garden hoses, but now it's out front on these metal racks. I suppose it makes sense for spring, but it just throws me off. I like things to stay where they belong, you know? Just like I'd like to feel normal again instead of jumping at every little shadow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, it's really taken a toll. I’ve missed so many shifts lately, and honestly, I've lost so much patience with the trainees. They just don't seem to grasp the importance of the checks. Because of all that, they don't even trust me to mentor the younger inspectors anymore. And I used to love doing that. I also had to pull back from the community theater. I just can’t reliably get through the rehearsals without needing to leave or feeling completely overwhelmed. 

It’s a shame about the theater, really. We were supposed to put on "Arsenic and Old Lace" this fall. I was going to help with the props. I love finding all those little vintage items to make the set look authentic. I found this gorgeous antique tea set at a garage sale last summer for only five dollars! The woman selling it didn't know what she had. It was genuine bone china, with these delicate little painted violets on the cups. I brought it home and washed it so carefully. You can't put something like that in the dishwasher, you know. The heat will just ruin the glaze. My daughter-in-law puts everything in the dishwasher, even her good kitchen knives. I keep telling her it dulls the blades, but she never listens to me. She just says she doesn't have time to stand at the sink washing dishes. I don't understand young people today, always in such a rush. They don't take the time to appreciate the little things, like a properly set table or a quiet afternoon with a good cup of tea. I used to drink Earl Grey every afternoon, but lately, caffeine just makes me too jittery. But anyway... yes, it's been very hard at work and with my activities. I just can't seem to manage like I used to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because... I just keep snapping at people. It's like I have no fuse left. Just the other day at the safety meeting, I started yelling before I could even stop myself. And it's not like I want to be that way, you know? I've been an inspector for almost thirty years, right out of high school. Back then, they used to have these huge metal sorting bins, not the plastic ones they use now. You really had to watch your fingers with those metal ones, I saw a guy lose a nail once... anyway, now the younger inspectors act scared to even ask me anything. They just look at me with these wide eyes and scurry off. It's just... I don't know why I get so mad so fast.\n\nDoctor: Has there been anything significant that's changed in your life recently, such as a new job, a move, or a problem in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't moved or anything. I've been in the same house for fifteen years. My husband planted these big blue hydrangeas out front when we first bought the place, and they just take over the whole walkway if you don't prune them right. You really have to cut them back in October or they go completely wild... But, um, as for work, no new job. I've been at the plant forever. \n\nThough I guess... well, all this anger and snapping, it really started about 2 years and 4 months ago. They forced this overnight schedule change on us. I never liked the night shift, you know? The break room is always freezing, and the vending machine is completely empty by 2 AM except for those stale peanut butter crackers. I always tell the vendor to put more of the trail mix in, but he never listens. Anyway, right after they switched my schedule to overnights, that's when that machinery accident happened. It's just been going on since then. I don't understand why management messes with the shifts to begin with, honestly. My sister works a set day shift at the bakery and she never has to deal with her hours bouncing all over the place...\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your behavior or activities besides getting angry more easily since the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess things have changed a lot. I’ve been missing a lot of shifts, to be honest. And like I was saying about snapping at people, I’ve just lost so much patience with the trainees that management actually told me I’m no longer trusted to mentor the younger inspectors. Which kinda stings, because I used to be the best trainer they had! Back in the day, we had these big, heavy yellow binders for the training manuals, and you could actually highlight the important safety codes. Now? They hand these kids iPads. Half the time they drop them right on the concrete warehouse floor. Do you know how much it costs the company to replace those glass screens? It's just ridiculous, they should get the heavy-duty rubber cases, but they don't listen to me. \n\nBut anyway... yeah, I just can't deal with the trainees right now. And I guess outside of work, too... I’ve completely pulled back from my community theater group. I just can’t reliably get through the rehearsals anymore without having to leave. It's a real shame because we were going to put on *Arsenic and Old Lace* this spring, and I was in charge of the props. Finding decent vintage teacups is actually a lot harder than you'd think. You have to drive around to all the different thrift stores on a Saturday, and even then, most of the good ones have chips on the rim, or they don't have the matching saucers...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about work or specific tasks when you're not supposed to be working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I don't sit around thinking about sorting or testing when I'm at home. When I clock out, I try to leave it at the plant. But it's hard sometimes because my husband is always watching those home renovation shows, you know? The ones where they buy a house sight unseen and then act surprised when the foundation is cracked. I always tell him, you have to get a proper inspection! You can't just slap some paint on a crumbling wall and call it an open concept. My cousin bought a house like that once, out in the suburbs, and the basement flooded the very first time it rained. Ruined all her old photo albums. She had this whole box of pictures from our trip to the Grand Canyon back in '92... such a shame. But anyway, no, I don't really think about my specific work tasks when I'm off the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or ashamed about how you've been behaving?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. I mean, I feel awful about snapping at people, but it's more than that. It's the accident. I keep thinking... if I had just pushed harder about the safety checks that night, or if I had just stopped the line when that run didn't feel right, maybe he wouldn't have gotten hurt so bad. It just eats at me. \n\nAnd you know, safety checks used to be so much more thorough. When I first started, we had this supervisor, Mr. Henderson. Oh, he was a stickler. He wore these thick glasses and always had a clipboard with a metal clip that would snap so loud it made you jump. He made us check the calibration on the sorting belts every two hours, on the dot. Now they just rely on the computer to tell them if something is off, but computers glitch all the time! Just last week, my home computer decided to do an update right in the middle of me trying to order new curtains for the living room. I had picked out this beautiful sage green pattern, and poof, the screen went black. I had to call my nephew to come fix it, and he said it was a motherboard issue, whatever that means. He works in IT for a hospital, so he knows all about that stuff. But honestly, if a computer can't even handle a curtain order, how can we trust it with heavy machinery? It just doesn't make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about the machinery accident that happened after the schedule change?\n\nPatient: Oh, all the time. It's not even that I'm trying to sit there and think about it, it just... happens. Like, if I hear a loud clank from a metal cart, or if I happen to smell hot oil somewhere, I am instantly put right back in that bay. I see my coworker right there by the machine, exactly like it’s happening in front of me all over again. It's just awful. \n\nBut you know, speaking of hot oil, my neighbor Mrs. Gable actually started making her own fried donuts on Sunday mornings. The smell drifts right over our shared fence. She uses this massive antique cast-iron Dutch oven that belonged to her grandmother. She swears by it, says it holds the heat better than anything modern, but I always tell her she needs to be so careful with deep frying at home. My uncle used to fry turkeys for Thanksgiving back in the early 2000s, and one year the oil boiled over and caught his wooden back deck on fire. Burned the whole railing right off! They had to replace the entire deck with that new composite wood stuff. It's supposed to be weather-resistant and never give you splinters, but honestly? It gets so incredibly hot on your bare feet in the summer. I tried walking on it during their Fourth of July barbecue and I had to run back to the grass to put my sandals on. I had just bought these really nice orthotic sandals from a mail-order catalog, the kind with the adjustable velcro straps, because my arches have been acting up lately...\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I go completely out of my way now just so I don't have to pass the inspection bay at the plant. I’ll take the long way around through the shipping department, even though it adds a good ten minutes to my walk. And I really won't talk about the accident if I can help it, I just change the subject. I even quit going to those industrial-history talks I used to enjoy so much at the community center. Seeing all those slides and pictures of the old machinery... it just sets me off now. \n\nWhich is a real shame, honestly, because they always had the best snacks at those talks. They’d bring out those little Danish butter cookies in the blue metal tins, you know the ones? My mother always kept her sewing supplies in one of those exact tins. I can't tell you how many times as a kid I opened it up hoping for a cookie and just found a bunch of tangled thread and one of those little tomato pincushions. She was always mending something, my mom. She had to hem all my jeans because I'm a bit on the shorter side, and finding pants that fit right off the rack is just impossible. Even now, I have to buy the 'petite' length from the department store, but half the time they're still too long, especially if I'm just wearing my everyday flats. I bought a pair of navy blue flats last spring for my niece's wedding, and they pinched my toes so badly by the time they served the cake... I had to take them off under the table. It was a beautiful cake, though, three tiers with lemon raspberry filling. But anyway, yeah, I just stay away from the bay and those history talks now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself trying to avoid thinking about the accident itself?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying... I really do try not to think about it. If someone brings it up, I just change the subject as fast as I can. And I take the long way around through shipping just so I don't have to look at the inspection bay, because looking at it makes me think about it. And stopping those history talks... it's all just to keep my mind off it. \n\nBut you know, walking through shipping has its own problems. They leave those wooden pallets everywhere, and my goodness, the splinters! The other day, one of the forklift drivers, Tommy—he’s a nice kid, just got his license last year, his mom works in accounting—he accidentally knocked a whole stack of them over. Sounded like a bomb went off. I swear, they need to sweep better back there. My husband used to be so particular about sweeping the garage. He had this special push broom with the stiff red bristles, and he’d sweep every Saturday morning right after he drank his coffee. He always drank it out of this ugly brown mug he got at a hardware store grand opening in, oh, it must have been 1998. They were giving away free hot dogs, too. I remember because I dropped mustard right down the front of my favorite white blouse. I tried everything to get that stain out—club soda, dish soap, even that expensive spray from the infomercials—but it never fully came out. I ended up having to wear it under sweaters for the rest of the winter so nobody would see the yellow spot. It was such a shame, it had these lovely pearl buttons... but anyway, yes, I just try to block the whole accident out of my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by loud noises or unexpected movements?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I'm easily startled, no. I mean, I don't really jump out of my skin if someone drops a book or anything like that. I guess I haven't really noticed being particularly jumpy. \n\nThough, speaking of jumping, my sister's dog—this little terrier mix she rescued from the shelter down on Elm Street—he jumps at absolutely everything. The poor thing is terrified of the mail carrier. Every day at 11:30, right when the mail comes through the slot, he just loses his mind. Barks and spins in circles. My sister tried buying one of those special mail catchers, you know, the baskets that attach to the inside of the door? She ordered it online, but when it arrived, the screws were missing. Can you believe that? You pay good money for shipping and they can't even put all the hardware in the box. She had to drive all the way out to the big Home Depot on the highway just to find the right size screws. And that parking lot is a nightmare, especially on a Saturday. People just leave their carts wherever they please. I saw a runaway cart hit a brand new silver sedan once—put a huge dent right in the passenger door. The owner was so mad, he was practically yelling at the poor teenage cart attendant who was just trying to gather them all up. It's a tough job for those kids, out in the heat all day. But anyway, no, I don't think I startle easily myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's exhausting, honestly. Even when I'm just sitting in the break room, or when I go to choir rehearsal on Thursday nights, I'm constantly watching the doors. I'm always listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. It's like my body just won't believe I'm safe, no matter where I am. \n\nWhich is really frustrating at choir, because I need to be watching the director, Mrs. Higgins. She’s been directing the choir for, oh, it must be twenty-five years now. She used to teach music at the elementary school before she retired. She has this habit of tapping her baton on the music stand when we're flat, and let me tell you, the altos have been terribly flat lately. I think it's because Mary Anne hasn't been coming to practice. She had knee surgery last month—a full replacement—and she's doing her physical therapy at that new clinic over by the mall. The one with the big glass windows? I always wonder how they keep those windows so clean. They must have to hire someone to come out with a squeegee and one of those long poles every week. I tried cleaning my living room windows last spring with newspaper and vinegar, because I read in a magazine that it doesn't leave streaks. Well, let me tell you, it left terrible streaks, and my hands were completely covered in black ink. I had to scrub them with baking soda just to get it off before my husband's boss came over for dinner. I made a pot roast for that dinner, the one with the little pearl onions and baby carrots. I always get the carrots from the farmer's market when I can, they just taste sweeter, you know? But anyway, yes, I'm always on edge, watching everything around me, even when I shouldn't be.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience flashbacks of the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I do. It's awful. Just the other day, I heard a clank from a cart down the hall, and then I caught a whiff of hot oil from somewhere, and suddenly... I was right back in that bay. I could see my coworker by the machine, just like it was happening right in front of me all over again. My heart was pounding so hard. \n\nIt’s funny how smells do that, isn't it? Like how the smell of cinnamon always makes me think of my grandmother's kitchen. She used to bake these incredible cinnamon rolls every Christmas morning. We'd wake up early, and the whole house would just smell like sugar and spice. She had this old cast-iron skillet she baked them in, must have weighed ten pounds. She always said the secret was in the pan, that it held the heat better than those flimsy aluminum things you buy at the supermarket nowadays. I tried to buy a cast-iron pan once at a flea market, but it was completely rusted over. The man selling it wanted twenty dollars for it! I told him he was out of his mind, charging that much for a piece of rusty metal. He had all sorts of junk at his table, old license plates, broken watches, even a stuffed owl that looked like it had seen better days. I don't know who buys that kind of stuff, really. I prefer to keep my house uncluttered. My husband, though, he's a bit of a packrat. He keeps every issue of National Geographic he's ever received. They're stacked up in the basement, taking up half the wall. I keep telling him to donate them to the library, but he says he might want to read them again someday. As if he has time to read forty years' worth of magazines! But anyway, yes, those moments... they just take me right back to the accident, like I'm there all over again.\n\nDoctor: Do you often blame yourself for what happened in the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It just eats at me. I keep thinking if I had pushed harder about the safety checks, or just stopped that run when it didn’t feel right, maybe he wouldn’t have gotten hurt so bad. It’s my job to catch those things, you know? \n\nBut speaking of checking things, it’s just like how they do the vehicle inspections now. My husband took our Buick in on Tuesday for its annual sticker, and they failed it over the wiper blades! Can you believe that? Wiper blades. I mean, I know you need to see in the rain, but he had just bought those blades at AutoZone not three months ago. They were the expensive ones, too, the ones with the special rain-repellent coating. The boy at the counter said they'd last a year, easy. It just goes to show you can't trust what salespeople tell you. Just like that time we bought the refrigerator from Sears before they closed down at the mall. The ice maker broke within a week. We had to call the repairman out three times, and he always tracked mud right onto my kitchen floor. I had just put down new linoleum, too. It looked like real hardwood, but it was so much easier to clean. You just run a damp mop over it with a little Pine-Sol. I absolutely love the smell of Pine-Sol, it just smells so fresh and clean, you know? It reminds me of spring cleaning when I open all the windows. But... what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, I just can't stop thinking I should have stopped that machine.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on high alert, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I think we just talked about this, didn't we? But yes, it's all the time. Like I said, even when I'm just sitting in the break room at work, or at choir rehearsal, I'm always watching the doors. I'm constantly listening for any sudden machine noises, and I catch myself checking to see who's moving around behind me. My body just refuses to believe I'm safe. \n\nIt's exhausting, really. Just like trying to keep up with my garden this year. The weeds have been absolutely terrible. I think it's all the rain we got in April. I spent three hours out there on Saturday pulling crabgrass, and my lower back has been aching ever since. I have this little foam kneeling pad, but it doesn't help much anymore. I bought it at the hardware store downtown—the one owned by the Miller family. Old Mr. Miller used to give out peppermint candies to all the kids when they came in. He passed away a few years ago, bless his heart, and his son took over. The son doesn't give out candy, though. He rearranged the whole store, and now I can never find the potting soil. It used to be right in the back corner by the garden hoses, but now it's out front on these metal racks. I suppose it makes sense for spring, but it just throws me off. I like things to stay where they belong, you know? Just like I'd like to feel normal again instead of jumping at every little shadow.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant difficulties in your daily life or relationships due to your feelings or behaviors since the machinery accident?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it's really taken a toll. I’ve missed so many shifts lately, and honestly, I've lost so much patience with the trainees. They just don't seem to grasp the importance of the checks. Because of all that, they don't even trust me to mentor the younger inspectors anymore. And I used to love doing that. I also had to pull back from the community theater. I just can’t reliably get through the rehearsals without needing to leave or feeling completely overwhelmed. \n\nIt’s a shame about the theater, really. We were supposed to put on \"Arsenic and Old Lace\" this fall. I was going to help with the props. I love finding all those little vintage items to make the set look authentic. I found this gorgeous antique tea set at a garage sale last summer for only five dollars! The woman selling it didn't know what she had. It was genuine bone china, with these delicate little painted violets on the cups. I brought it home and washed it so carefully. You can't put something like that in the dishwasher, you know. The heat will just ruin the glaze. My daughter-in-law puts everything in the dishwasher, even her good kitchen knives. I keep telling her it dulls the blades, but she never listens to me. She just says she doesn't have time to stand at the sink washing dishes. I don't understand young people today, always in such a rush. They don't take the time to appreciate the little things, like a properly set table or a quiet afternoon with a good cup of tea. I used to drink Earl Grey every afternoon, but lately, caffeine just makes me too jittery. But anyway... yes, it's been very hard at work and with my activities. I just can't seem to manage like I used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
